> - **B - retry the insert inside the promotion on a unique violation.** Needs a savepoint around the insert and a recompute, and the exception is raised at commit/flush time in this code path; more moving parts than A for the same result.
> - **C - number assigned by the database** (a `BEFORE INSERT` trigger or sequence-per-document). Strongest guarantee, independent of code paths; needs a migration and a trigger that itself takes the per-document lock; larger than needed.
> - **D - leave the number alone but make a collision cheap:** treat the unique violation as retryable without counting an attempt and skip the rescan. Reduces the cost, keeps the race, adds special-casing to the state machine.
> - **Recommendation: A** (with A2 as an optional follow-up). Required tests: the two-transaction numbering IT above (both succeed with consecutive numbers, current/latest end on the later commit, no failed ticket); a unit test that `persist` numbers from the row it reads rather than from the plan; a gate-level IT with two concurrent uploads of one document asserting both clean, one scan each, no `DOCUMENT_SCAN_FAILED`. Possible regressions: code that reads the number out of the object key (none found), the `VersionItem` list and exports that show the number (they read the row).
> - **Proposed files if A is approved:** `document/DocumentVersionPromoter.java` (persist; prepare's comment/hint), `document/DocumentPromoter`'s javadoc in `scanning/service/DocumentPromoter.java` (the plan's number is a hint), and new tests (a promoter-level concurrency IT next to `DocumentVersionPromotionGuardIT`, a unit test, a gate-level IT). No migration, no frontend change, `docs/scanning-architecture.md` one sentence.
> - **The embedded-signature gap (item 3) would need its own decision:** re-reading the current stored file inside `persist` (the guard's file read currently uses the request's tenant, so it would need a tenant-explicit form), or recording embedded signatures at upload so the records-based check sees them. Not part of this finding; not proposed here.
> **Limitations / untested:** the constraint violation, the retry after it and the ticket wording were not run against a database; the contention estimate for large files and bulk import is an inference; the signing-flow consequence is from code reading; the embedded-signature gap is verified in code only.

### DOC-09 · Purge vs restore race destroys files of restored document (Medium) – FAIL
- `document/RecycleBinService.java:368,457,193`: no re-check of `is_deleted`/legal hold before `destroyFiles`; TOCTOU also for legal hold placed after the check.

> **Status: FIXED – TESTS PASSED** (2026-11-11; option A implemented, see §17 DOC-09). The investigation below is kept as written; it was recorded as INVESTIGATING before the fix. The original finding above is kept unchanged.
>
> **Investigation of DOC-09 - confirmed behaviour, code-based inference, untested**
> - **Conclusion: CONFIRMED, reproduced on real infrastructure.** Severity Medium is right (irreversible data loss, narrow trigger).
> - **Root cause (verified in code).** `RecycleBinService.purge` (`:457-491`) is not one atomic step: it (1) loads the versions, (2) **destroys every version's file, preview, thumbnail and OCR artefacts** through `destroyFiles` (`:465`, `:501-549`) with no transaction and no lock, and only then (3) in a separate `transactions.executeWithoutResult` (`:469-480`) marks the versions `purged_at`, deletes `document_metadata`, `document_access`, relationship links and bookmarks, and runs `update documents set purged_at = now() where document_id = ?` - **with no condition on `is_deleted`, `purged_at` or `legal_hold`**. Every check that decides whether to purge happens earlier and is stale by then: `purgeNow` (`:224-237`: `inBin`, edit level, `isLegalHold`, `hasProtectedVersion`), `emptyBin` (`:244-268`: reads up to 5000 `Document` entities once, `MAX_EMPTIED` `:71`, and tests the stale flags per item), and the hourly `purgeExpired` (`:345-380`: a SQL snapshot of up to 200 ids - `PURGE_BATCH` `:70` - then `findById(...).ifPresent(purge)` `:368`, no re-test of `is_deleted`). `restore` (`:193-221`) is `@Transactional`, finds the document with `findInRecycleBin` (`is_deleted and purged_at is null`), clears `is_deleted` and commits, taking no lock that a purge in flight would respect. The window is the time to delete all files (longer on object storage and with many versions), and for `emptyBin` the age of the up-front entity snapshot (up to the whole run).
> - **Reproduced (real PostgreSQL 5433 in a new disposable database, real services and file storage, real `RecycleBinService`/`LegalHoldService`; an isolated probe class compiled outside the repository into the git-ignored `target/test-classes`, run alone via failsafe, then removed; database dropped). The purge was paused deterministically after `destroyFiles` returned (a spy on `OcrArtifactWriter.deleteFor`, its last step) and before the marking transaction; the competing action ran in that gap; nothing relied on timing.**
>   - **A - restore during a purge:** after the pause: document `is_deleted=true`, file already gone. `restore` then **succeeded** (audit `DOCUMENT_RESTORED` 1). The purge then returned normally. End state: **`is_deleted=false` and `purged_at` set on the document, version `purged_at` set, file gone**, audit has both `DOCUMENT_RESTORED` and `DOCUMENT_PURGED` (1 each). The user view: `getDocument` still returns the document (title shown), `downloadDocument` fails with `ResourceNotFoundException: DocumentVersion not found`. The document looks live and is empty.
>   - **B - legal hold after the restore:** same pause; `restore` then `LegalHoldService.place` both succeeded (placement is only possible on a live document: `placeLegalHold` requires `deleted = false`, `DocumentRepository:56-60`); the purge then completed: **`legal_hold=true`, `purged_at` set, version purged, file destroyed** - the "never destroy a held document" rule (V127, `purgeNow` `:227-231`, sweep `:358`) is bypassed.
>   - **C - control (sequential):** purge then restore -> `ResourceNotFoundException`; purge of a bin document works and audits once - the normal path is fine.
>   - All three probe tests passed asserting the defect. A and B are real-database, real-storage results; they exercise `purgeNow` (the single-document purge). **`emptyBin` and the sweep call the same `purge()` and were not run separately** (conclusion from code).
> - **Legal-hold TOCTOU is a sub-case of the restore race, not independent (verified in code).** A document in the bin cannot be put on hold (`placeLegalHold ... deleted = false`) and a held document cannot be deleted (`deleteDocument` runs `editGuard.guard`, `DocumentServiceImpl:577-579`). So a hold can only arrive after a restore - exactly scenario B. Protected versions (`document_versions.is_protected`, V32:124) have **no setter in application code** (grep: only the column and read-only checks), so that variant is reachable only through direct database changes; it is covered by the same fix anyway.
> - **Only one path un-deletes a document:** `RecycleBinService.restore` (`:203`; the other `setDeleted(false)` hits are tenants and users). The version-retention restore (`VersionRetentionService:207`) concerns versions, not documents.
> - **Impact.** Data integrity and availability: the content of a document the user has just restored is destroyed for good (files, previews, OCR text, encryption keys), while the system presents it as a normal live document with no downloadable version. By code (not run): the purge transaction also deletes the live document's `document_metadata`, `document_access`, relationship links and bookmarks (`:473-478`), and a purged-but-live row has no recovery path (versions tombstoned). Also a compliance impact in B (a held document's files destroyed). Not a security/authorization hole: tenant isolation is intact (`inBin` and the purge use the tenant of the document; probe was single-tenant), permission to restore or purge is unchanged. Requires two privileged actions (restore and purge/empty-bin/sweep) on the same document within the purge window; asynchronous processing contributes (the sweep runs hourly on its own thread; `emptyBin` can run for minutes).
> - **Existing protections and their limits.** The check-then-destroy order and the "destroy first, mark purged only after" design make a failed purge retryable (comment `:533-542`) - it does not protect against a state change. The sweep's Postgres advisory lock (`:347`) only prevents two sweeps at once, not a sweep against `restore`, `purgeNow` or `emptyBin`. `findInRecycleBin` and `restore`'s own check protect only the sequential case. No DB constraint ties `purged_at` to `is_deleted` (grep of the migrations). `RecycleBinService.destroyFiles` deleting an already-gone object is a no-op, which is what makes a lock-then-destroy design safe to retry.
> - **Existing tests.** `DocumentUploadIT` (`recycleBin_restoreBringsItBack...`, `...emptiesAroundAHold...`, the sweep/warn/flag tests), `DocumentCheckoutIT` (`:383-396`, hold vs sweep, sequential), `BookmarkIT`, `RecordsRetentionIT`, `OwnershipOffboardingIT`: **all sequential. No test interleaves a purge with a restore or a hold; none asserts that a purge refuses a document that is no longer deleted.**
> - **Relationship to previous fixes (nothing modified).** DOC-01: soft-delete is enforced on reads (`deleted = false`), so the purged-but-live document passes `getDocument` and then fails on download - the end state is exactly what DOC-01's checks cannot see. DOC-02/03/04: content serving, limits and headers are unaffected. DOC-05/06/07: version-promotion rules read `deleted`, holds and check-outs, not `purged_at`; a restored-and-purged document would still accept a new upload under those rules (inference, not run). DOC-08: numbering reads all version rows including tombstones - no interaction. The embedded-signature concurrency gap is unchanged and not part of this finding.
> - **Related but separate (not part of DOC-09, not proposed here):** `VersionRetentionService.destroyDue` (`:127-146`) has the same pattern - it selects versions `not d.legal_hold`, calls `destroyFiles`, then marks them - and a hold can be placed on a live document in between (verified in code, not run). The `LegalHoldService` class comment (`:21`, "version purge / recycle-bin purge do not yet look at this flag") is stale. Recorded for a FOLLOW-UP.
>
> **Remediation options (nothing implemented)**
> - **A - one transaction under a document row lock (recommended).** `purge` becomes a single transaction: first `select ... for update` of the document row **where it is still `is_deleted`, not purged, not on hold**, re-testing the legal hold and the protected-version rule under the lock (otherwise skip/refuse with the existing conflict messages); then `destroyFiles`, then the existing updates, all in that transaction. `restore` takes the same lock (a new lock query for in-bin documents; `lockByDocumentIdAndTenantId` filters `deleted = false`) and re-checks `purged_at is null`. Whichever starts first wins: restore first -> the purge's locked re-check finds a live document and skips it; purge first -> `restore` waits, then reports the document gone; a hold placed meanwhile waits on the row and then matches nothing. All three callers (`purgeNow`, `emptyBin`, `purgeExpired`) go through `purge`, so one change covers them. Failure behaviour is unchanged: if file destruction throws, the transaction rolls back and the document stays in the bin for the next sweep; if the commit fails after files were destroyed, a rerun is safe (deleting a gone object is a no-op). No migration. Pros: closes the race completely for restore, hold and protection with the smallest change; reuses the lock idiom of DOC-06. Cons: a database connection and the document's row are held while files are deleted (restore of that one document waits for it; on slow object storage with many versions this is seconds), and the empty-bin and sweep loops should treat a skipped document as skipped, not failed; it enlarges the "storage I/O inside a transaction" concern recorded as DOC-12.
> - **B - claim first, destroy afterwards.** A short transaction locks and re-checks the document and sets a marker (a new state/column such as `purge_started_at`, or reuse of `purged_at` with a separate "files destroyed" flag); `restore` and hold placement refuse a marked document; the files are then destroyed outside any transaction and a retry job finishes interrupted ones. Pros: no long lock, no I/O in a transaction. Cons: needs a **migration**, a new state in every query that tests `purged_at`, and a recovery job (today's "mark only after success" retry property would be given up or re-implemented); much larger.
> - **C - re-check immediately before destroying, no lock.** Smaller than A, but it only narrows the window - the restore can still land between the re-check and the deletion - so the defect remains. Not recommended.
> - **D - only make the final update conditional** (`... where is_deleted and purged_at is null`). Prevents the live-and-purged row but **not the destruction of the files**, which has already happened; it would turn silent corruption into a failed purge of a restored document whose files are already gone. Not sufficient.
> - **Recommendation: A.** It is the smallest change that stops the destruction itself rather than only detecting it afterwards, with no schema change. Required tests (real database, real transactions, no sleeps, using the same pause-in-`destroyFiles` technique): restore during a purge (purge first -> restore refused/not found and the file gone is consistent; restore first -> purge skips and files, rows, access, links and bookmarks remain); legal hold after a restore -> purge skips; `emptyBin` and `purgeExpired` skip a document restored after their snapshot and count it as skipped; a purge whose file deletion fails rolls back and leaves the document in the bin; the sequential paths keep passing (`DocumentUploadIT` and `DocumentCheckoutIT` recycle-bin tests, `BookmarkIT`, `RelationshipLifecycleIT`). Possible regressions: callers that expect `purge` to be non-transactional (the `transactions` template is used inside it today), notification and audit calls (should stay after commit), lock ordering against other writers of the document row (restore/hold/delete/promotion all lock this row alone, so no cycle is expected - to be re-checked at implementation).
> - **Files potentially changed if A is approved:** `document/RecycleBinService.java` (purge, restore, the callers' skip handling), `document/DocumentRepository.java` (a lock-in-bin query), the new tests (next to `DocumentUploadIT`), and `docs` (one note); `LegalHoldService`'s stale comment optionally. No migration, no frontend change.
> - **Remaining uncertainties:** `emptyBin` and `purgeExpired` were not exercised concurrently (same `purge`); the loss of metadata, access, links and bookmarks for a live document is from the code, not observed; how long the window is on the production object store (SeaweedFS) was not measured; behaviour under the sweep's advisory lock with several application instances was not tested; whether to also fix `VersionRetentionService.destroyDue` is for a separate decision.

### DOC-10 · Bulk import trusts ZIP entry sizes (Medium) – FAIL
- `importexport/service/ImportArchiveIntake.java:100-108` passes `entry.getSize()` as content length; `BulkImportServiceImpl.receiveFile` (:395-460) batch cap/recorded size use the declared value. Forged small size bypasses `max-batch-size`.

> **Status: FIXED – TESTS PASSED** (2026-11-11; option A implemented, see §17 DOC-10). The investigation below is kept as written; it was recorded as INVESTIGATING before the fix. The original finding above is kept unchanged.
>
> **Investigation of DOC-10 - confirmed behaviour, code-based inference, untested**
> - **Conclusion: CONFIRMED, reproduced on real PostgreSQL and real file storage, with one correction to the original wording** (below). Severity Medium is reasonable (authenticated, permission-gated, availability and accounting; no confidentiality or tenant-isolation impact).
> - **Root cause (verified in code).** In the ZIP intake, `ImportArchiveIntake.unpack` (`:99-153`) takes the entry's size from `ZipEntry.getSize()` (`:121`, `:129`), which is read from the ZIP's central directory - a field the uploader writes - and passes it to `BulkImportService.receiveFile` as the content length together with the entry's stream. `receiveFile` (`BulkImportServiceImpl:395-466`) then (1) applies the batch cap to that declared number before reading a byte: `stagedBytes + contentLength > max-batch-size` (`:414-418`); (2) streams the entry into staging storage with the declared length (`:445-446`, rollback cleanup `:447`); (3) records the size the **storage layer reports** (`item.setStagedSizeBytes(stored.sizeBytes())` `:457`), which the cap's running total (`ImportItemRepository.sumStagedBytes` `:37-38`) later sums. Nothing counts the bytes that actually arrive. The JDK's `ZipFile.getInputStream` for a deflated entry does not enforce the central directory's size, so an entry that claims a few bytes can inflate to gigabytes.
> - **Correction to the original text:** the recorded size is not always the declared value. It depends on the storage layer: `FileSystemStorageAdapter.upload` ignores the length and returns the real `Files.size` (`:51-64`); `S3ObjectStorageAdapter.upload` returns the declared length (`:96-124`); `AzureBlobStorageAdapter.upload` returns the declared length (`:72-87`); and `EncryptingObjectStorageService.upload` - **encryption is enabled by default** (`EncryptionProperties.enabled = true`) - records and returns the declared size as the plaintext size (`:74-134`, `row.setPlaintextSize(size)`). So with default settings the recorded size is the forged one, and the running total stays wrong for the rest of the batch.
> - **Reproduced (real PostgreSQL 5433 in a new disposable database; real `ImportArchiveIntake`, `BulkImportServiceImpl`, repositories, real file-system staging storage with the default encryption wrapper, real ZIP parsing; an isolated probe class compiled outside the repository into the git-ignored `target/test-classes`, run alone through failsafe, then removed; database dropped). Setup: `max-batch-size` lowered to 1 MB inside the test and restored; a batch with two rows (`big.pdf`, `small.pdf`) created through the repositories (the CSV template path was not used).**
>   - **Mechanics (JDK only):** an 8 MiB deflated entry whose central-directory size field was edited to 100: `ZipEntry.getSize()` = 100, compressed 8,172 bytes, **actually inflated 8,388,608 bytes**.
>   - **Control (honest ZIP, same file):** refused - "Adding this file would take the import over its 1MB limit." - nothing staged. The cap works when the size is truthful.
>   - **Forged ZIP:** the entry was **accepted** (`filesMatched` 1, no problems). The item shows `staged_size_bytes = 100` while the staging storage holds **8,388,608 bytes** (cap 1,048,576). `sumStagedBytes` for the batch = 100. A following honest 2 KB file was **accepted** as well - the cap is bypassed for the rest of the batch, not once.
> - **Not verified (needs a real S3/SeaweedFS or Azure target, not started):** what happens when the declared length is smaller than the stream. A JDK-level check of the AWS SDK shows `RequestBody.fromInputStream(1000 bytes, contentLength=10).contentStreamProvider()` hands out all 1000 bytes (the provider does not truncate), so whether the HTTP layer sends a short body, fails, or the server rejects it was **not** established; the encryption wrapper also computes the ciphertext length from the declared size. Expect an error or a corrupt/truncated object on S3-style backends - **inference only**; the file-system backend (reproduced) stores everything.
> - **Where it is reachable.** `POST /api/v1/bulk-imports/{batchId}/archive` (`BulkImportController:124-133`, multipart) and `POST /api/v1/bulk-imports/{batchId}/archive/from-upload-session` (`:135-141`, resumable upload), both `@PreAuthorize FIE_BULK_IMPORT_UPLOAD_FILE`. The single-file endpoint `POST .../files` (`:113-122`) passes `file.getSize()`, the size of what the server actually received, so it is not affected. The batch is looked up and locked by tenant and creator (`lockByImportBatchIdAndTenantIdAndCreatedBy` `:402`), so another tenant or user cannot be reached. No concurrency is needed; the batch lock only serialises entries.
> - **Impact (availability / accounting / data accuracy).** (1) The 20 GB per-batch staging cap (`max-batch-size`, `application.yml:603`) can be exceeded without a practical bound: the ZIP itself is limited only by its own size (`max-archive-size` 20 GB, `:609`) and entry count (`max-archive-entries` 5,000), and deflate ratios of about 1000:1 are normal for zero-filled data, so one batch can stage far more than the cap - staging storage exhaustion by an authenticated user holding the upload permission (by design only a bounded number of rows per batch, `max-files-per-batch` 500, and one active batch per user, which limit the count, not the size). (2) The recorded size is wrong (here 100 for an 8 MiB object), which the worker later passes on as the declared size when it hands the staged file to the scan gate (`ImportRecordCreator:267-273`); how the gate and the resulting document then record the size was **not run** (inference: wrong `size_bytes` on the version). (3) No document content is corrupted on the file-system backend and no cross-tenant or permission boundary is crossed. Because the declared size is also used for the early cap check, an honest-looking small header is enough; no malformed archive is needed.
> - **Existing protections and limits.** `max-archive-size` (real size of the uploaded ZIP), `max-archive-entries` (`unpack` `:108`, central directory count), the cap on declared sizes, the file-type check and refusal of nested archives/encrypted files (`receiveFile` `:425-431`), the intake virus scan (`IntakeVirusScanner`, and a second scan at record creation), per-user single active batch, row cap, tenant+creator-scoped batch lock. **The scan gate already does this correctly for ordinary uploads** - `SizeLimitingInputStream` (`scanning/helper/SizeLimitingInputStream.java`) enforces the limit "on the bytes that actually arrive, not the declared Content-Length" (`IngestionGateImpl:135`, `:157-170`) - but its limit is `max_file_size_bytes`, unlimited by default (`application.yml:690`), as already noted under DOC-03, and it is applied only when the file reaches the gate, after the bulk intake has staged it. The DOC-03 archive checks (`ZipArchiveCheck`) apply to ZIPs uploaded as documents; bulk import never ingests the ZIP, so they do not cover its entries.
> - **Existing tests.** `ImportArchiveIntakeTest` (unit, mocked service) has no test with an entry whose header size differs from its content; `BulkImportServiceImplTest` (unit, mocked storage) tests the cap only with truthful declared lengths (`:143`) and verifies the declared length is passed through to storage (`:393`); there is **no integration test of the archive intake or of bulk-import staging against a real database or storage**, and no test of the size recorded after an upload.
> - **Relationship to previous fixes (nothing modified).** DOC-03 is the same class of defect (trusting archive metadata) in a different component and a different intake; its ZIP accounting is not reused here. DOC-01/02/04: no interaction (staged files are not served). DOC-05/06/07/08: the staged file later goes through `documentService.uploadDocumentFromJob` / `versions.upload`, i.e. the normal gate and promotion rules, unchanged. DOC-09: staged-object cleanup (`StagedImportFiles`) is a different path. The embedded-signature race and the version-retention race are unrelated and remain open follow-ups.
>
> **Remediation options (nothing implemented)**
> - **A - measure the bytes in `receiveFile` (recommended).** In `BulkImportServiceImpl.receiveFile`, before the upload, compute the remaining budget (`max-batch-size - sumStagedBytes`) and wrap the content in a stream that counts what is actually read and fails as soon as it exceeds the smaller of the declared length and the remaining budget, or ends short of the declared length. On a violation the upload is aborted and the staged object removed by the existing rollback (`deleteOnRollback`); the entry is reported as a problem by the existing `catch (ApiException ...)` in `ImportArchiveIntake`. Record the **counted** size as `staged_size_bytes`, not `stored.sizeBytes()`. Pros: one place covers every caller (ZIP, upload-session ZIP, single file), the cap becomes true, the recorded size becomes true on every backend, S3/Azure get a truthful length or a clean error instead of a truncated object, the pattern already exists in the gate (`SizeLimitingInputStream` can be reused or a counting variant added), no migration. Cons: honest entries whose header differs from their content (rare, malformed ZIPs) are now refused with a clear message; a small new error code.
> - **B - validate the ZIP first.** Inflate each entry once to a temporary file (or to a counter) in `ImportArchiveIntake`, compare with the header, then stage. Catches mismatches before staging but doubles the I/O and disk use for large imports and still needs the budget check; larger and slower than A for the same outcome.
> - **C - cap the actual bytes only in `ImportArchiveIntake`** (wrap the entry stream at the declared size). Smaller change, but it leaves `receiveFile` trusting its `contentLength` for any other caller and does not fix the recorded size; it also gives no early budget bound. Acceptable as a stop-gap, not complete.
> - **D - add a cumulative inflated-size limit for bulk ZIPs** like DOC-03's `ZipArchiveCheck`. Useful as a defence in depth against archives engineered to inflate, but not a fix on its own: it needs the same actual-byte measurement as A to be reliable, and `max-batch-size` already is that limit once measured.
> - **Recommendation: A.** Required tests: unit tests of the counting stream and of `receiveFile` with a stream longer and shorter than its declared length (cap enforced on actual bytes, counted size recorded, staged object removed, no row change); a real-database/real-file-storage IT of the ZIP intake (the probe's scenario: forged entry refused, honest control accepted, later honest file still judged against the true total, staged storage holds no stray object) plus the upload-session path; a regression check that the single-file endpoint and honest ZIPs behave as before; `ImportArchiveIntakeTest` for the problem message. Possible regressions: legitimate ZIPs whose entries use data descriptors or STORED entries with odd headers (the JDK reports real sizes from the central directory, so honest tools are unaffected - to be checked with ZIPs made by common tools), memory/streaming behaviour (the wrapper is a pass-through), and the encryption wrapper's use of the declared size (unchanged when the count matches).
> - **Files potentially changed if A is approved:** `importexport/service/impl/BulkImportServiceImpl.java`, a small stream helper in the importexport package (or reuse of `scanning/helper/SizeLimitingInputStream`), possibly `ImportArchiveIntake.java` (message), tests under `src/test/java/com/oak/dms/importexport/...` (unit and one IT in the `watermark` package to reuse its fixtures, or a new fixture), and `docs` (a short note). No migration and no frontend change.
> - **Remaining uncertainties:** behaviour on S3/SeaweedFS and Azure with a wrong declared length was not run; how the wrong recorded size shows up on the created document was not run; the CSV-template batch creation and the worker were not exercised (the batch was seeded through repositories); the effect of a very large inflated entry on the intake virus scan (ClamAV limits) was not tested; very large sizes were not tried - the cap bypass was shown with 8 MiB against a 1 MiB cap.

### DOC-11 · External I/O under DB transaction with row lock (Low/Medium)
- `BulkImportServiceImpl` class-level `@Transactional` (:100); `receiveFile` holds a pessimistic batch lock during upload + virus scan (:445-453).

> **Status: FIXED – TESTS PASSED** (2026-11-11; option A implemented, see §17 DOC-11). The investigation below is kept as written, including the two corrections to the DOC-09 record; it was recorded as INVESTIGATING before the fix. The original finding above is kept unchanged.
>
> **Investigation of DOC-11 - confirmed behaviour, code-based inference, untested**
> - **Conclusion: CONFIRMED, reproduced on real PostgreSQL and real storage, and worse than the original text says** - the held transaction does not only serialise one batch, it can exhaust the shared connection pool and, because the same request needs a *second* pooled connection while it holds the first, make the starvation self-sustaining until timeouts fire. Line numbers have moved since the audit (DOC-10 edited the method): the class-level `@Transactional` is now `BulkImportServiceImpl:101`, `receiveFile` `:396-498`.
> - **Root cause (verified in code).** `BulkImportServiceImpl` is `@Transactional` for the whole class (`:101`), so `receiveFile` runs in one transaction that starts when the method is entered. Its first statement takes the batch row lock (`lockByImportBatchIdAndTenantIdAndCreatedBy`, `PESSIMISTIC_WRITE` = `select ... for update`, `:403`, repository `ImportBatchRepository:26-31`), and the lock and the pooled connection are kept until commit. Inside that window it does, in order: the file-type check, the **storage upload** (`:459`, through the encryption wrapper, to the staging store - file system, S3/SeaweedFS or Azure), on a failure a storage delete (`:468`), the **virus scan** (`:485`, `IntakeVirusScanner.scan` -> the engine re-reads the object from storage and streams it to clamd, whose socket read timeout is the engine's `timeout_seconds`, 900 s by default, `ScanProperties:29`, `ClamAvScanAdapter:312`), and only then the item update (`:494`). The comment at `:400-401` shows the lock is deliberate (one upload at a time per batch, so a row cannot be claimed twice and the size cap cannot be slipped under); the cost is that external I/O of unbounded duration sits inside a database transaction. Contrast: `cancel` (`:591-613`) already defers its object deletes until after commit (`clearAndDeleteAfterCommit`), and the import worker is written with short transactions on purpose (`BulkImportWorker` class comment, `claim` `:186-215`).
> - **Reproduced** (real PostgreSQL 5433 in a new disposable database, real services, real file-system staging storage behind the default encryption wrapper, real Hikari pool of the application (max 10, connection timeout 20 s - `application.yml:23-26`); an isolated probe class compiled outside the repository into the git-ignored `target/test-classes`, run alone through failsafe, then removed; database dropped). Setup: two batches seeded through the repositories; the first `receiveFile` is given a stream that stalls after 12,000 bytes, i.e. inside the storage upload (a stand-in for slow storage or scan); stalls and releases are latch-controlled, nothing relies on timing except a 1.5 s pause used only to show the transaction age.
>   - **One in-flight upload:** its database session is `idle in transaction` (age 1.6 s at the observation), the pool shows 1 active connection. Expected and as found.
>   - **More uploads into the same batch** (9 further `receiveFile` calls, the pool size minus the holder): all 9 sat **waiting on the row lock** (`wait_event_type = 'Lock'`, count 9) and the pool went to **active=10, idle=0** - a request that is waiting for the lock still holds a pooled connection, because its transaction is already open. One user with ten parallel requests into one batch therefore takes the entire pool.
>   - **With the pool exhausted:** an unrelated `select 1` through the application's own pool and an upload into a **different** batch both blocked (`threadsAwaitingConnection = 2`), and both then failed with `Connection is not available, request timed out after 20000ms (total=10, active=10, idle=0, waiting=2..3)`. An application **scheduled task (`OutboxRelay.relayPendingEvents`) failed the same way** (logged "Unexpected error occurred in scheduled task"). So the starvation is application-wide, not limited to the batch.
>   - **Self-sustaining:** after the stall was released, the holder did not finish: it needed another connection and timed out after 20 s, its error handling (`removePartlyStagedObject` -> `EncryptingObjectStorageService.delete` -> `ObjectKeyRecords.find`) needed yet another and timed out after another 20 s (stack in the log). The reason is in the code: `ObjectKeyRecords.find/save/delete` are `@Transactional(propagation = REQUIRES_NEW)` (`encryption/ObjectKeyRecords:23,28,33`), so every encrypted upload, download, scan read and delete **opens a second connection while the outer transaction still holds the first**; `IntakeVirusScanner` also opens a `REQUIRES_NEW` transaction when it blocks a file (`:65`). When all pooled connections are held by outer transactions, none of them can get the inner one: the classic pool-starvation deadlock, broken only by Hikari's 20 s timeout. The exact first call that needed the extra connection after the release was not identified from the log; the REQUIRES_NEW mechanism and the two timeouts in the holder's thread are verified. The test errored at its final assertion because of these timeouts (an outcome of the finding, not of the probe); the evidence above was captured before that.
>   - Not run: a real clamd scan stall, a real slow object store, more than one application instance, the infected-file path that takes the inner `REQUIRES_NEW` transaction, a pool larger than 10.
> - **How long the window is (inference from code, not measured).** A multipart request is fully received by Spring before the controller method runs, so a slow *client* does not hold the transaction; the transaction lasts for the server-side work: the storage upload (network to SeaweedFS/S3 for large files, plus encryption), the re-read for scanning, clamd's scan (up to the 900 s read timeout per socket operation), and for a ZIP the inflating of the entry (`ImportArchiveIntake.unpack` is not transactional itself; each entry's `receiveFile` is, `:99-153`). Large files make this minutes, not milliseconds.
> - **Reachable by whom.** Endpoints `POST /api/v1/bulk-imports/{batchId}/files`, `.../archive` and `.../archive/from-upload-session` (`BulkImportController:113-141`), permission `FIE_BULK_IMPORT_UPLOAD_FILE`, tenant+creator-scoped batch (no cross-tenant reach). There is **no bulk-import screen in the front end** (searched `frontend` source; only permission codes appear), so only API clients call these. No request-rate or concurrency limit on these endpoints was found in the application or in the repository's gateway configuration (searched for `limit_req`, `limit_conn`, rate-limit classes; the only limiters are login and OCR-token ones) - inference, a deployed proxy may differ. The `max-active-batches-per-user` limit (1) does not limit parallel requests.
> - **Impact.** Availability, with a data-integrity side effect: (1) a held row lock blocks `submit` and `cancel` of that batch (`:507`, `:597`) and the worker's `claim` for as long as an upload runs; (2) a pooled connection per in-flight or waiting request - ten are enough to stop all database access for the whole service for 20 s at a time (verified), including schedulers such as the outbox relay; (3) an upload that starves rolls back and is rejected although the file was fine, and its cleanup may itself fail (`removePartlyStagedObject` logs an error and attaches the failure, so an orphaned staged object is reported, not hidden); (4) long `idle in transaction` sessions hold back vacuum. No unauthorised access, no tenant-boundary effect, no corruption of accepted data. **Severity: the original Low/Medium stands** - the evidence supports the upper end for availability under concurrent use, but it needs an authenticated caller with the upload permission and API-level parallelism, recovery is automatic after the timeouts, and nothing is lost or exposed; I would not raise it without a measured production-like load.
> - **Existing protections and limits.** The batch lock itself (correctness: no double-claim, no cap overshoot, no cancel/submit interleaving with an in-flight upload); object clean-up on rollback (`deleteOnRollback` `:781`) and on a failed upload (DOC-10, `:468`); Hikari's 20 s timeout turns a deadlock into an error instead of a hang; per-batch scope of the lock; the worker's short transactions. No lock timeout, no concurrency limit and no transaction timeout was found (`application.yml`, searched for `lock_timeout`, `default-timeout`, `idle_in_transaction`).
> - **Relationship to previous fixes (nothing modified).** DOC-10 added work in the same window (counting, a post-upload completeness check, a storage delete on failure) - it neither created nor reduced the problem; its error handling now performs a second storage call (a `REQUIRES_NEW` key lookup) inside the transaction on failure. **DOC-09 is the same class**: `RecycleBinService.purge` holds a transaction, the document row lock and a pooled connection while it deletes files, and its storage deletes go through the same `REQUIRES_NEW` key records, so many concurrent purges could starve the pool the same way (inference; not tested; purges are far rarer and mostly sequential - `emptyBin` and the sweep run one document at a time). DOC-05/06/07/08 (promotion transaction), DOC-01/02/04 (reads) and DOC-03 are unaffected. The embedded-signature race, the version-retention race and the S3/Azure/retry follow-ups remain separate; the S3 retry caveat after DOC-10 is related only in that an upload retry would run inside this same transaction.
> - **Two corrections to earlier records of mine, found while tracing this (not edited in place):** (a) the DOC-09 entry says its concern about "storage I/O inside a transaction" is "recorded as DOC-12"; **that finding is DOC-11** (DOC-12 is the WOPI heap buffering finding). (b) The DOC-09 entry says the encryption key-row deletes "join" the purge transaction and "come back with the rollback"; that is wrong: `ObjectKeyRecords` is `REQUIRES_NEW`, so key rows are deleted in their own committed transactions and are **not** rolled back with a failed purge. The DOC-09 safety conclusion is unaffected (the restore check asks storage whether the object is readable, and a deleted key row plus a deleted object reads as missing), but the sentence should be corrected, and it means a half-failed purge also leaves its keys already destroyed.
> - **Existing tests.** `BulkImportServiceImplTest`, `BulkImportReceiveFileSizeTest` (unit, mocked repositories and storage - no transaction, lock or pool behaviour), `BulkImportSizeEnforcementIT` (DOC-10; real database and storage, sequential). **No test covers concurrent uploads, the batch lock, `submit`/`cancel` during an upload, connection-pool behaviour, or the nested `REQUIRES_NEW` connection.**
>
> **Remediation options (nothing implemented)**
> - **A - take the I/O out of the transaction, keep the invariants by checking again under the lock (recommended).** `receiveFile` runs outside a transaction (`@Transactional(propagation = NOT_SUPPORTED)` on the method, with an injected `TransactionTemplate`) and does three steps: (1) a short transaction: lock the batch, check it is `uploading`, match the row, read the budget, commit and release; (2) with no transaction, no lock and no connection held: type check, upload (to a key unique to this attempt), count and verify (DOC-10), virus scan; (3) a short transaction: lock the batch again, re-check `uploading`, that the chosen row is still unstaged and that the verified size still fits the cap, then record the item and commit; if any check now fails (a cancel, a submit, a concurrent upload for the same row, a cap race) the object is deleted and the request is refused with the existing error. Nothing is persisted to claim a row or reserve budget, so **no migration**; the cost is that a race is discovered after the upload work instead of before it (wasted work, never a violated invariant), and the staged key is per attempt instead of per row (`StagedImportFiles` and the worker already use the key stored on the row, not a rebuilt one). Removes the lock hold, the connection hold and - because there is no outer transaction - the nested-connection deadlock on this path. Cons: moderate restructuring of one method (the constructor gains a transaction manager; unit tests that construct the service change), the "uploads into one batch are serialised" behaviour becomes "uploads run in parallel and conflicts are rejected", and a duplicate filename listed on several rows needs a clear rule when two uploads race for the same row (the second is refused and retried by the client).
> - **B - bound the damage without changing the transaction (interim).** A small non-transactional gate in front of `receiveFile` (the controller and `ImportArchiveIntake`): a per-batch in-process lock so same-batch requests queue without holding a connection, plus a global semaphore sized well below the pool (each in-flight upload may need two connections: at most about (pool - reserve) / 2), with a bounded wait and an explicit "busy, retry" response; optionally a `lock_timeout` on the batch lock so a waiter that does get through fails fast. No migration, no change to the invariants, easy to test with the same latch technique. But the transaction, the row lock and the connection are still held across the I/O (so `submit`/`cancel` still wait), the limits are per instance, and the nested `REQUIRES_NEW` hazard remains for the connections that are held.
> - **C - persist a claim, then work lock-free** (a status or columns on `import_items` such as `staging_started_at`, reserved bytes; reserve under a short lock, upload, finalise under a short lock, a sweeper for abandoned claims). Strongest guarantees against wasted work and the cleanest semantics, but needs a **migration**, a new state in every query that looks at staging, and a recovery job; larger than the confirmed defect needs.
> - **D - make the nested key-record calls join the caller's transaction** (remove `REQUIRES_NEW`). Would remove the second-connection hazard everywhere, but it changes the encryption module's rollback semantics (a key row is deliberately committed before the object it protects exists) and affects every caller; out of scope and risky. Not recommended.
> - **Recommendation: A** (B only if you prefer to defer the restructuring). It is the only option that fully removes the confirmed cause - external I/O under a transaction and a row lock - without a migration or a change to what the encryption module guarantees. Required tests (real PostgreSQL, real storage, latches, no sleeps, `pg_stat_activity` and the Hikari MXBean to observe): while an upload is stalled in storage, the batch row is **not** locked (`submit`, `cancel` and a second upload to another row complete) and **no pooled connection is held**; more concurrent stalled uploads than the pool size, and an unrelated query still completes; two uploads racing for one row - exactly one staged, the other refused with its object deleted; two uploads that fit the cap alone but not together - the total never exceeds the cap and no stray object remains; `cancel` and `submit` during an upload - the object is removed, the batch ends in the right state; a failure in each step cleans up; the DOC-10 size checks and the existing sequential tests keep passing; unit tests for the three steps. Possible regressions: key naming of staged objects, `StagedImportFiles` clean-up for batches staged before the change, the worker's claim/lock ordering, the "already received" error contract for parallel clients, tests that build the service directly.
> - **Files potentially changed if A is approved:** `importexport/service/impl/BulkImportServiceImpl.java` (receiveFile, key builder, constructor), `BulkImportServiceImplTest` and `BulkImportReceiveFileSizeTest` (construction and flow), a new real-database IT (in the `watermark` package, reusing its fixtures), `docs/scanning-architecture.md` (the DOC-10 paragraph mentions the lock). Probably `ImportBatchRepository` unchanged. No migration, no frontend change.
> - **Remaining uncertainties:** how long real uploads and scans hold the transaction on SeaweedFS/S3 and with a loaded clamd (not measured); which call first needed the extra connection after the release in the probe; behaviour with a larger pool or several application instances; whether a deployed proxy rate-limits these endpoints; the DOC-09 purge starvation hazard is inferred, not tested; the infected-file `REQUIRES_NEW` path was not run.

### DOC-12 · WOPI save buffers up to 200 MB in heap; token in query string (Low/Medium)
- `editing/WopiController.java:172`. Preview paths hold whole PDFs (`document/DocumentRenditionService.java:638`, cache ≤64 MB/entry) (Low/Medium).

> **Status: FIXED – TESTS PASSED for the confirmed defect (PutFile heap buffering)** (2026-11-11; option B implemented, see §17 DOC-12). The other parts of the original wording are handled as the investigation found: GetFile already streams (unchanged), the query-string token is a protocol-level residual (unchanged), PDF previews are bounded, and the unguarded image-preview branch is a separate follow-up (not changed). The investigation below is kept as written; it was recorded as INVESTIGATING before the fix. The original finding above is kept unchanged.
>
> **Investigation of DOC-12 - labelled: CONFIRMED FROM SOURCE / CONFIRMED BY MEASUREMENT / INFERRED / NOT VERIFIED**
> - **Conclusion: PARTIALLY CONFIRMED, and split.** (1) **WOPI PutFile buffers the whole save in heap - confirmed from source and by measurement**, with a 2x transient allocation. (2) **WOPI GetFile is NOT affected - it streams in constant memory (confirmed by measurement)**; the original wording did not claim otherwise, but it is recorded so nobody "fixes" it. (3) The **access token in the query string** is real but is the WOPI protocol's own mechanism and is contained (see below). (4) The **preview paths** hold whole PDFs by design, bounded; one unguarded branch (images) found. Line numbers have moved: the cited `WopiController.java:172` is still the `readNBytes` line; `DocumentRenditionService:638` is now `:650-662`.
> - **WOPI call graph (CONFIRMED FROM SOURCE).** All three are under `@RequestMapping("/internal/v1/wopi/files/{documentId}")` (`WopiController:71`), `permitAll` in `SecurityConfig` (the signed token is the credential; `CookieBearerTokenResolver:41-43` deliberately ignores the `Authorization: Bearer` header on this prefix), and unreachable from outside: `infra/production/nginx/dms.conf:121` answers 444 for `/internal/`; the engine reaches dms-core on the internal network.
>   - **CheckFileInfo** `GET /{documentId}` - `WopiController.checkFileInfo` `:90-125` -> `contentReader.currentForEditing` `DocumentContentReader:132-137` (`@Transactional(readOnly)`, metadata only). No file bytes.
>   - **GetFile** `GET /{documentId}/contents` - `getFile` `:127-147`: `authorize` (token HMAC + document id match + tenant editing switch, `:223-234`) -> `live` (same tenant, not deleted, `:237`) -> `contentReader.openCurrentForEditing` (`DocumentContentReader:139-142`, **`@Transactional` covers only the lookup and opening the stream; it commits before a byte is sent**) -> `VersionContent.open` (`:36-71`: purged, tenant, `document` purpose, scan verdict, then `storage.download` `:64-65`) -> `StreamingResponseBody` `in.transferTo(out)` `:134-138`, `Content-Length` = `DocumentVersion.size_bytes` (`VersionContent:69`). **Streaming end to end:** file system `Files.newInputStream`; S3 `ResponseInputStream` (`S3ObjectStorageAdapter:187-201`); Azure `BlobInputStream`; the encryption wrapper decrypts through Tink `AesGcmHkdfStreaming` in 64 KiB chunks (`ObjectCipher:64-70`, `EncryptingObjectStorageService.download`). No file-size limit; no watermark or conversion on this path. Access to the *current user's folder* is deliberately not re-checked (`DocumentContentReader:120-126`).
>   - **PutFile** `POST /{documentId}/contents` - `putFile` `:149-205`: `authorize` -> `live` -> `canWriteNow` (`:242-256`: signature lock, legal hold, signature record, exclusive-mode check-out by this user / simultaneous-mode no foreign lock) -> autosave header short-circuits **before any body is read** (`:163-165`) -> `request.getContentLengthLong()` `> MAX_SAVE_BYTES` (200 MiB, `:76`, `:166-169`) is refused with 413 -> **`in.readNBytes((int) MAX_SAVE_BYTES + 1)` into a `byte[]` (`:170-173`)** -> length re-check `:174-176` -> filename lookup `:177` -> `EmbeddedSignatureDetector.hasSignature(filename, new ByteArrayInputStream(bytes))` `:181` (itself streaming: 64 KiB windows, `EmbeddedSignatureDetector:60-118`) -> `ingestionGate.submit(IngestionRequest.newVersion(..., bytes.length, new ByteArrayInputStream(bytes), ...))` `:185-187` -> audit `DOCUMENT_EDIT_SESSION_SAVED` `:195-199`. No database transaction is open in the controller; the gate streams the content onward (`IngestionGateImpl:157-170`, `SizeLimitingInputStream`) and uses short transactions internally.
> - **PutFile buffering facts (CONFIRMED FROM SOURCE).** Whole body in heap: yes, up to 200 MiB + 1 byte. A second full copy exists transiently inside `InputStream.readNBytes(int)` (it reads into a list of chunks and then allocates and fills the result array). The array stays referenced by the request thread for the entire `ingestionGate.submit` call (staging, scan, and for small files promotion). **Content-Length is not trusted for the read** (the read is capped at the constant; the servlet container itself enforces the declared length) and **chunked requests are accepted** (declared length -1 skips the early 413, the read cap still bounds it). **Actual bytes are counted** (`bytes.length`, passed to the gate as the length). No temporary file, no spooling, no partial object: nothing is stored until the gate runs, and the gate cleans up its own staging on failure. Authorization (token, live document, signature lock, hold, check-out) happens **before** the body is read; a request with a bad token never allocates. The 200 MiB figure is a hard-coded constant, not configurable, and is the **only** size limit on this path - the comment above it ("bigger than any editable Office file the upload limits already allow") does not match the defaults, where the ordinary upload limits are unlimited (`application.yml:54-55` `-1`, `:697` `Long.MAX_VALUE`). GetFile has no limit, so a document over 200 MiB that was uploaded normally can be opened in the editor but not saved back (413).
> - **Measured (CONFIRMED BY MEASUREMENT; private copy of the DOC-11 verification snapshot, whose six WOPI-related sources are byte-identical to the live tree; real `WopiController`, real PostgreSQL, real encrypted file storage; the gate was a stub that drains the stream, so only the controller's own memory behaviour is measured; request bodies were generated on the fly so the test held none of them; allocation = bytes allocated by the request thread, exact; heap peak = a 2 ms sampler, approximate).**

  | Scenario | Size | Allocated by the request | Notes |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | PutFile, Content-Length declared | 16 / 32 / 64 MiB | 32.3 / 64.3 / 128.4 MiB | **2.01-2.02 x size**; heap seen ~ +34 / +66 / +130 MiB |
  | PutFile, chunked (no Content-Length) | 32 MiB | 66.6 MiB | 2.08 x |
  | PutFile, 201 MiB, declared | 201 MiB | 0.1 MiB | refused 413 before reading |
  | PutFile, 201 MiB, **no Content-Length** | 201 MiB | **400.9 MiB** | 413 only after reading 200 MiB + 1 byte: **~400 MiB transient per request** |
  | GetFile (open + stream to a null sink) | 16 / 32 / 64 MiB | 2.4 / 2.2 / 2.2 MiB | **constant**, independent of size (0.03-0.15 x) |
  | 2 concurrent saves of 64 MiB, heap cap 900 MiB | 128 MiB total | heap in use 377.7 MiB while both held their files | both saved |
  | **8 concurrent saves of 64 MiB, heap cap 900 MiB** | 512 MiB total | heap in use 814.1 MiB | **3 of 8 failed with `OutOfMemoryError`**, 5 saved |
  | 8 concurrent GetFile of 64 MiB, mid-stream, heap cap 900 MiB | 512 MiB total | heap in use 154.6 MiB (baseline 115.3 MiB) | all 8 streamed |

> - **Memory-risk estimate.** *From the measurements:* a save needs ~2x its size transiently while it is read and ~1x until the gate returns; a chunked 200 MiB request costs ~400 MiB transient. *Extrapolation (INFERRED, not measured at this scale):* the production default heap is ~1.9 GB (`backend/Dockerfile:88` `MaxRAMPercentage=75`, `infra/production/docker-compose.yml:358` `mem_limit` 2560m, ~0.4 GB idle per the comment there), so roughly 5-7 simultaneous **maximum-size** saves, or a few dozen of typical 20-50 MiB office files, would exhaust it, and GC pressure begins earlier. The JVM stayed up in the probe, but a thread that hits `OutOfMemoryError` can be any thread of the service. No rate or concurrency limit exists on these endpoints (none found in the application or the repository's nginx files; they are not proxied at all).
> - **Who can trigger it (CONFIRMED FROM SOURCE unless marked).** Only a holder of a valid, unexpired WOPI token for that document *and* - for PutFile - while `canWriteNow` is true (exclusive mode: still the check-out holder; no signature lock, hold, or signature record). The token is HMAC-signed, bound to one tenant, user and document, lifetime `dms.editing.token-ttl` = 10 h (`EditingSessionService:73`, `WopiTokenCodec`). The route is not reachable from outside the platform network (nginx 444). So the realistic triggers are legitimate: several users saving large Office files at once, or the engine retrying; a malicious editor would need network access to dms-core's internal port (INFERRED: not tested). Tenant isolation holds (token tenant, `live()` tenant filter, `VersionContent` tenant/location checks; `WopiEditingIT` has a cross-tenant test). Confidentiality and integrity are not affected by the buffering itself; it is an **availability** issue.
> - **Transaction boundaries.** GetFile: the only transaction is the short `@Transactional openCurrentForEditing` (committed before streaming; the `TenantContext.clear()` in the `finally` also runs before the body, which does not need it); nothing is held during the transfer. PutFile: no transaction in the controller; the gate's own short transactions; **no pooled connection is held while the body is read or buffered**. (The DOC-11 class of problem does not apply here.)
> - **Token in the query string (CONFIRMED FROM SOURCE).** `access_token` is read only as a query parameter (`WopiController:92,129,152`). That is how WOPI works (the engine appends it to every call and also repeats it as `Authorization: Bearer`, which this host ignores on purpose, `CookieBearerTokenResolver:36-43`). The browser never carries it in a URL: the editor page posts it to the engine frame as a hidden form field (`frontend/components/checkout/document-editor.tsx:238-239`). Exposure is limited to the engine -> dms-core hop on the internal network: no Tomcat access log or request-logging filter is configured, `AuditValueRedactor` redacts `access_token` keys, nginx does not proxy the route. Residual: any internal log or proxy that records URLs would capture a token valid for up to 10 h, which allows **reading the current version** (GetFile re-checks only that the document is live and editing is on, not the user's folder access) and, while the check-out stands, saving. Whether the engine's own logs redact the token was NOT VERIFIED. Severity of this part: Low; mitigations would be a shorter lifetime or preferring the header; independent of the heap issue.
> - **Preview paths (second half of the original text).** CONFIRMED FROM SOURCE: `DocumentRenditionService.pdfBytes` `:650-662` reads the whole PDF (original or converted) into a `byte[]` with `readAllBytes` (`readAll` `:808-815`; again ~2x transient) and caches up to 4 PDFs of up to 64 MiB each (`:137-146`, `CACHED_PDFS`, `CACHED_PDF_MAX_BYTES`) - up to **~256 MiB retained permanently** per instance. The size is bounded: a PDF or convertible file over `dms.preview.partial-max-bytes` (default 150 MiB, `:174`) is refused `PREVIEW_TOO_LARGE` before it is read (`:607-612`, `:710-714`), and over `dms.preview.max-bytes` (50 MiB) only the first pages are rendered. These endpoints require a signed-in user with view access (not the WOPI token). **Gap found:** the **image branch** (`source` `:598-601`, extensions png/jpg/jpeg/gif/bmp/tif/tiff, `:113`) calls `readAll` **before** any size check, so a very large image is read whole. NOT MEASURED. The preview behaviour is a documented trade-off of the preview feature and is a separate remediation from WOPI.
> - **Existing protections and limits.** Early 413 on a declared oversize; read cap; authorization and state checks before the body; the gate's scan, type check and promotion rules (DOC-05/06/07/08: signature, lock, hold, check-out lease, numbering all apply at promotion); stream closed by try-with-resources on completion, error or client disconnect. No concurrency limit, no memory-aware limit, no test of the oversize/chunked paths. Not verified: **an abandoned GetFile** - the stream is opened in the handler and closed only inside the body, so if the async body never runs the stream is not closed (the regular download has the same shape and `DocumentDownloadStreamCleanupIT` covers completion, failure and disconnect, not "never started"); and the container's async timeout (no `spring.mvc.async.request-timeout` is set, so the Servlet container default applies - a very slow or very large GetFile might be cut off; not tested).
> - **Existing tests.** INSPECTED: `WopiEditingIT` (39 tests; the ingestion gate is a mock; covers token, tenant, state, autosave, signature refusal, small save, `getFile` bytes), `WopiTokenCodecTest`, `EditingEngineTest`. EXECUTED here: only the private probe above (not a repository test). **No test** covers the 413 paths, a chunked save, memory behaviour, concurrent saves, the real gate with WOPI, or GetFile stream cleanup.
> - **Relationship to previous fixes.** DOC-11 (no transaction/connection during I/O) is unaffected: neither WOPI path holds one. DOC-10 (byte counting) is the model for the missing piece: PutFile already counts actual bytes but only by holding them. DOC-02 (streaming downloads) is the pattern GetFile already follows; it needs no change. DOC-05/06/07/08/09 act at promotion and are unaffected. The embedded-signature race and the version-retention race are unrelated and remain open.
> - **Severity.** The original **Low/Medium stands - I would keep it at the Low end for exploitability and the Medium end only for availability under legitimate load**. Evidence for: 2x amplification and up to ~400 MiB per chunked request, and OOM at 8 concurrent 64 MiB saves under a 900 MiB heap (measured). Evidence against raising it: token-scoped, authenticated, check-out-bound, not reachable from outside, no confidentiality or integrity impact, the existing 200 MiB cap, and the production heap is ~2x larger than the probe's. A reproducible denial of service from outside was **not** shown and is not claimed.
>
> **Remediation options (nothing implemented)**
> - **A - end-to-end streaming of the PutFile body into the gate, no copy.** GetFile already does this. For PutFile the obstacle is the signature check, which must complete **before** `submit` (a clean small file is promoted inside `submit`, so a check after it would be too late) and currently needs the whole content. A single pass would need a sniffing stream that aborts mid-stream on a signature marker: practical for PDF (`/ByteRange`, already a raw scan) but for OOXML/ODF it would replace the exact ZIP-entry check by a raw search for `_xmlsignatures/` / `META-INF/documentsignatures.xml` (a superset: it can refuse a file that merely contains the text), i.e. a behaviour change to a security check; plus the gate then needs an exact length up front (chunked requests have none; S3 needs it, the encryption wrapper spools when it is unknown). Lowest memory, highest risk and most changes.
> - **B - bounded spool to a temporary file, then the existing flow (recommended).** Stream the body into a private temp file while counting actual bytes and enforcing the existing 200 MiB cap on the **actual** bytes (reuse `SizeLimitingInputStream` from `scanning/helper`, and `TempFiles` for the secure creation), then run `EmbeddedSignatureDetector` on the file (unchanged, exact), then hand the gate a stream over the file with the exact length, and delete the file in a `finally`. Heap per save becomes a few 64 KiB buffers regardless of size; the 413 behaviour for declared and undeclared oversize is kept (the undeclared one then costs disk, not heap); chunked requests keep working; every check and the order stay as they are. Costs: temp-disk space of up to 200 MiB per concurrent save, a plaintext temporary file (consistent with what the platform already does: Spring's multipart handling spools ordinary uploads to the temp directory and `EncryptingObjectStorageService` spools unknown-length uploads to `TempFiles`), cleanup after a crash (files left under the temp directory; an age-based sweep or a dedicated subdirectory), and ~2 more disk passes per save.
> - **C - bounded concurrency in front of the current code (a semaphore sized from the heap).** Protects the heap without touching the data path, but only per instance, rejects valid saves under load (the engine would report a failed save), still allocates 2x per accepted save, and needs tuning. A possible add-on to B for disk (not needed for heap), not a fix by itself.
> - **D - a WOPI-specific smaller cap.** Not justified: the 200 MiB cap already exists, the product needs to support large documents, and lowering it would break saves that work today. Do not recommend. (Making the existing constant configurable, and aligning its comment with the real limits, is a possible small addition.)
> - **Recommendation: B.** It removes the heap exposure completely without changing any security check, the authorization order, the error responses, the version-creation path or the cap, and it needs no migration. A is cleaner in memory terms but changes the signature check; C only hides the problem. GetFile and the DOC-02 download code stay untouched.
> - **Proposed boundaries if B is approved.** `editing/WopiController.putFile` (replace `:166-187`); a small helper in the `editing` package (spool + count + cap + cleanup, using `TempFiles` and `SizeLimitingInputStream`); no storage-interface, encryption, transaction or migration change; the temp file is deleted in a `finally` and a leftover-file sweep is a decision to take (dedicated subdirectory plus age-based cleanup at startup is the smallest); `docs/checkout-design.md` gets a short note. Optional, separate and not part of B: the image-branch size guard in `DocumentRenditionService.source`, a header-preferred or shorter-lived token, GetFile stream cleanup when the body never runs.
> - **Required tests if B is approved (real PostgreSQL, real storage, deterministic).** Heap: PutFile of N MiB allocates a bounded amount independent of N (the same exact `ThreadMXBean` allocation measure as the probe), for declared and chunked bodies; 201 MiB declared -> 413 without reading, 201 MiB chunked -> 413 after exactly the cap with no heap growth and the temp file removed; N concurrent saves under a small heap all succeed. Behaviour unchanged: every `WopiEditingIT` case; a save carrying a signature is still refused (PDF, OOXML, ODF) and nothing reaches the gate; a gate refusal / scan failure / exception removes the temp file; an exception while spooling removes the temp file and stores nothing; a client that disconnects mid-body leaves no file; the version created is byte-identical to the body (hash) and its size is the counted size; autosave still stores nothing and reads no body; lock/hold/signature changes mid-session still stop saves; tenant and token checks unchanged; the audit event is unchanged. Use the **real** ingestion gate in at least one test (the existing IT mocks it).
> - **Remaining uncertainties / NOT VERIFIED:** whether the real Collabora sends a Content-Length on PutFile (assumed, not verified); the real production heap behaviour and the number of concurrent saves in practice (extrapolated); the async timeout effect on very large GetFile; the abandoned-GetFile stream leak (inferred); whether the engine's own logs redact the token; the image-preview memory use (read from source only); no measurement was made with the real ingestion gate or at the 200 MiB scale beyond the oversize case.

### DOC-13 · Orphaned partial/pending objects (Low)
- `scanning/helper/StorageObjectMover.copyVerified:58-66` (no destination cleanup if upload throws); `storage/adapter/FileSystemStorageAdapter.java:48-57`; `scanning/service/impl/IngestionGateImpl.java:196-204,224-228` (`UncheckedIOException` bypasses `failTerminal/purgeStaging`; row stays pending until retry scheduler).

> **Remediation status: FIXED – TESTS PASSED** for the confirmed exception-driven defects (2026-11-12; option A implemented, see §17 Remediation Log). Crash-related and historical orphans are not addressed (listed there). The investigation below is kept as written; it was recorded as INVESTIGATING before the fix. The original finding above is kept unchanged.
>
> **Investigation of DOC-13 - labelled: CONFIRMED FROM SOURCE / CONFIRMED BY TESTS / INFERRED / NOT VERIFIED**
> - **Conclusion: PARTIALLY CONFIRMED, and split into three distinct behaviours of different weight.** Severity Low stands for (1) and (3); (2) is a real but small consistency defect.
> - **(1) Orphaned partial/unreferenced objects in the document location - CONFIRMED FROM SOURCE and BY TEST, and permanent.** `StorageObjectMover.copyVerified` runs `target.upload(...)` and only deletes the destination on a hash mismatch; if the upload throws (a `StorageException`), nothing is cleaned up. `IngestionProcessor.promote` cleans the destination only for failures after `copyVerified` returns. The retry (`ScanRetryScheduler` -> `process`) calls `DocumentVersionPromoter.prepare` again, which generates a **new** version id and object key, so the failed destination object is never revisited. Nothing garbage-collects the document location (`docs/scanning-architecture.md` already says "storage waste only; Phase 2 GC"). **Test (private probe, real gate/processor/PostgreSQL/file storage/ClamAV, copy failing after 100 bytes):** the ticket stayed `pending` with the failure recorded, a 40-byte partial object stayed at `documents/<tenant>/<doc>/v2/<id>`, and after the retry promoted the upload under a different key the partial object was still on disk with no `document_versions` row pointing at it (3 files on disk, 2 referenced). Same pattern in `QuarantineReleaseService.overrideRelease` (copy with no cleanup if the copy itself throws) and `isolate` (deterministic quarantine key, so a retry overwrites it - self-healing there).
> - **(1b) Storage adapters differ.** `FileSystemStorageAdapter.upload` writes straight to the final path (`CREATE, WRITE, TRUNCATE_EXISTING`) and leaves the partial file there on `IOException` (confirmed from source and by the test above); it also truncates any existing object at that key. S3 uses one `putObject` with a declared length and Azure `uploadWithResponse`, so partial objects are not expected there (inferred from the adapter code; NOT tested against real S3/Azure). `EncryptingObjectStorageService.upload` (default on) calls `undo()` on a `RuntimeException`, which restores/deletes only the key record, **not the object**, so a failed encrypted upload leaves undecryptable ciphertext plus no key record (confirmed from source; the test above ran through this wrapper and saw the leftover file).
> - **(2) Gate steps after the staging upload bypass the failure handling - CONFIRMED FROM SOURCE and BY TEST, but self-healing.** In `IngestionGateImpl.submit` only `catch (StorageException)` around `stagingStore.upload` does `deleteQuietly` + `failTerminal` + `purgeStaging`. A failure in the ZIP/OOXML check (`UncheckedIOException`, or a `StorageDownloadException` from `download`, which is not caught at all), in `recordStaged`, or elsewhere leaves the row `pending` with a live lease and the staged object present. **Test:** a ZIP upload whose first staging read failed -> `submit` threw `StorageDownloadException`; the row stayed `pending` (hash recorded, lease live, staged file present); when the scheduler picked it up it **scanned and promoted the file** (version count 1 -> 2, staging purged). So the caller was told "error" while the document was created anyway - an inconsistency (a client retry would create a second version or hit the duplicate check), not a security or data-loss defect.
> - **(3) Partial staging object when the staging upload fails with an unchecked exception - CONFIRMED BY TEST, bounded.** The upload failed with an `UncheckedIOException` (before `recordStaged`): row `pending`, no hash, partial staged file; when the scheduler picked it up it went to `failed` ("Upload was interrupted before staging completed") via `failTerminal`, which does not purge. The staged file stays until the `failed` retention sweep (`findStagingToPurge`, default 72 h): the probe showed the row is **not** due with a 72 h retention and **is** due with zero retention. So staging leftovers are reclaimed after about 3 days; only the document-location orphans in (1) are permanent.
> - **Impact.** Storage waste and an inconsistent error/outcome in rare failure windows (storage I/O error, process crash, ZIP read failure). No tenant, authorization or integrity effect: orphans are unreferenced, unreachable through the API, and never served. Document-location orphans accumulate without bound over the years only if storage failures are frequent. Severity: **Low**.
> - **Existing mitigations.** Lease + `ScanRetryScheduler` (30 s) re-claims abandoned pending rows; `failed` staging is purged after 72 h; clean/quarantined staging is purged after processing; hash-mismatch deletes the destination; promotion failures after the copy delete it; S3/Azure writes are atomic.
> - **Test gaps.** No test for `StorageObjectMover`, no unit test for `IngestionGateImpl`, no partial-write test for `FileSystemStorageAdapter`; the gate is only exercised on its success/refusal paths by integration tests.
> - **Remediation options.**
>   - **A - cleanup at the failure points (recommended).** `copyVerified` deletes the destination (best effort) on any exception from `upload`; `overrideRelease` and `promote` inherit it; `IngestionGateImpl.submit` wraps everything after the pending row exists in one catch for `RuntimeException`/`UncheckedIOException`: `deleteQuietly(staging)`, `failTerminal`, `purgeStaging`, then rethrow (so the outcome matches what the caller is told). Small, no migration, no schema or API change. Cons: best-effort delete can itself fail (a crash leaves the object), so it does not cover process death.
>   - **B - atomic writes in the filesystem adapter** (write to a temp name in the same directory, then atomic move; delete the temp on failure). Removes partial files at the source for local storage and stops truncating an existing object; leaves the encryption-wrapper `undo` gap and crash leftovers (temp files need a sweep). Medium size; adapter-level, affects every FS write.
>   - **C - garbage-collect unreferenced document-location objects** (a sweeper comparing storage listing with `document_versions`/key records). Covers crashes and every historical orphan, but needs a storage `list` operation, a safety window against in-flight promotions and a legal-hold review; the largest change and riskiest (deleting live data on a bug). It is the already-planned "Phase 2 GC".
>   - **D - leave as is and document.** Reasonable only if storage faults are rare; keeps the inconsistency in (2).
> - **Recommendation: A, plus the encryption-wrapper `undo` also deleting the object it wrote (small, same spirit); B and C as separate follow-ups if the product wants crash-proof cleanup.**
> - **Proposed boundaries if A is approved.** `scanning/helper/StorageObjectMover`; `scanning/service/impl/IngestionGateImpl.submit` (one catch after row creation; no change to the success path, duplicate/type refusals or lease logic); optionally `EncryptingObjectStorageService.undo`. No change to `IngestionProcessor` retry logic, `ScanRetryScheduler`, sharing, the promoter, or storage interfaces; no migration.
> - **Required tests if A is approved.** Unit: `StorageObjectMover` (upload throws -> destination delete attempted and the original exception propagates; delete failure does not mask it; hash mismatch unchanged; success unchanged). Real-DB gate ITs: ZIP-check failure, `recordStaged` failure and a staging download failure each -> row `failed`, no staged object, caller gets the error, no later promotion; partial-copy failure -> no leftover destination file and the retry promotes cleanly (the three probe scenarios turned into assertions). Regression: DOC-05/06/07/08/10/11/12 integration classes that go through the gate and promoter, plus scanning/quarantine ITs and the full unit suite.
> - **Dependencies / risks.** Touches the ingestion gate used by uploads, versions, WOPI saves and bulk import, so the regression set must be run; the catch must not swallow the "duplicate/refused" ordinary results or turn the lease-expiry retry path into double processing. Cleanup after a failure must not delete a staged object that the processor may legitimately still claim - hence `failTerminal` first.
> - **NOT VERIFIED:** real S3/Azure partial-write behaviour; crash (kill -9) leftovers beyond the simulated exceptions; Linux file semantics; how frequently storage faults occur in production; whether the production default encryption wrapper is enabled in every tenant. The probe ran in a private copy of the code against a throwaway database and temp storage; it has been removed and is not part of the repository.

### DOC-14 · Folder move/create races (Low, unverified)
- `folder/FolderService.java:174-197,238-247`: no lock; concurrent opposite moves could create a cycle; unverified whether a unique index backs `requireFreeName`.

> **Remediation status: FIXED – TESTS PASSED** for the confirmed folder-tree races (2026-11-12; options A + D implemented, see §17 Remediation Log). The document-side placement race (point 4 below) is a separate follow-up and is not fixed. The investigation below is kept as written; it was recorded as INVESTIGATING before the fix. The original finding above is kept unchanged.
>
> **Investigation of DOC-14 - labelled: CONFIRMED FROM SOURCE / CONFIRMED BY TEST / INFERRED / NOT VERIFIED**
> - **Conclusion: CONFIRMED (the move race), with two related races of the same cause and one part of the original wording resolved.** Folder structure changes are decided by reading the tree and then writing, in READ COMMITTED transactions, with no lock between the two (`FolderService` has no lock, no `FOR UPDATE`, no advisory lock, no version column; the only database guards are the sibling-name index and "not its own parent").
> - **Resolved: the sibling-name index exists** (confirmed from source and by test). `V51__create_folders.sql:41` creates `uq_folders_live_sibling_name` (tenant, parent or all-zero for the top level, `lower(folder_name)`, `WHERE NOT is_deleted`); `FolderService.save` (`:284-291`) turns the violation into the same 409 `FOLDER_NAME_TAKEN`. A direct insert of `SAME` next to `Same` was rejected with `DataIntegrityViolationException`. So the "unverified whether a unique index backs `requireFreeName`" part is answered: create, rename and move cannot produce duplicate live sibling names; only the friendlier pre-check is racy, which is harmless.
> - **(1) Opposite moves create a cycle - CONFIRMED BY TEST.** `move` (`FolderService.java:173-197`) reads `repository.findSubtreeIds(tenantId, folderId).contains(newParentId)` (`:181`) and only afterwards saves (`:194`, `saveAndFlush` in `save`). Each transaction sees only committed data, so `move(A under B)` and `move(B under A)` both pass; the database has no cycle guard (`V51:33` only forbids parent = self; no trigger). **Test (private probe, real service, real PostgreSQL, the race window held open with row locks so both moves have finished their check and queue at the UPDATE, then released):** both calls returned OK; the rows ended `A parent=B`, `B parent=A`; **0 of 2 folders reachable from the top level** (they disappear from the tree together with their documents; the flat `listAll` still returns both rows); `path(A)` still terminates (its `treePath` has a visited-set); and the move-cycle query itself (`FolderRepository.findSubtreeIds`, `:36-45`, `WITH RECURSIVE ... UNION ALL`) **does not terminate on the cycle** - it was cancelled by a 3 s statement timeout. The application sets no statement timeout (none in `application.yml`), so a later move of a cycle member, or a bulk export of its subtree (`ExportDocumentSelector.java:95`), would run an unbounded query holding a connection (inferred from the same SQL; the real service call was not left running).
> - **(2) Depth limit bypassed - CONFIRMED BY TEST.** `create` (`:143`) and `move` (`:185`) check `treePath(parent).size() + depthBelow(...) >= MAX_DEPTH (20)` against committed data. Two moves that are each allowed alone (a 5-deep subtree `p..p4` moved under a depth-15 folder: 15 + 4 < 20; a leaf `z` moved under `p4`: 5 + 0) both committed together: **`z` ends at depth 21**. Consequence is limited to the 20-level rule (the tree walks stop at `MAX_DEPTH`, so `path`/`treePath` of such a folder is cut short).
> - **(3) Create versus delete of the parent - CONFIRMED BY TEST.** `delete` (`:199-227`) checks "no live children" (`:204`), "no documents" (`:210`) and "no pending uploads" (`:218`) and then soft-deletes (`:222-225`); `create` (`:137-156`) checks the parent (`:142`) and inserts (`:148`). **Test:** with the delete paused after its checks (before commit), a `create("Child", parent)` committed; the delete then committed: **`Parent deleted=true`, `Child deleted=false`** - a live folder under a deleted parent (invisible in the tree; `folders.is_deleted` is a soft flag, so the composite foreign key does not object).
> - **(4) Documents filed into a folder that is being deleted - INFERRED FROM SOURCE, NOT RUN.** The same check-then-act shape exists on the document side: `DocumentServiceImpl.uploadDocument` checks the folder at request time (`:196`) and the promoter writes `folder_id` from the scan result (`DocumentVersionPromoter.java:128`) without re-checking that the folder is still live; `moveDocuments` (`DocumentServiceImpl.java:386-412`) reads the folder and updates the document. Mitigation that exists: `delete` refuses while a `pending`/`infected` scan result targets the folder (`:218`, `countPendingUploads`), which closes the long upload window; what remains is the short window between the request-time check and the insertion of that pending row, and `moveDocuments` against `delete`.
